The (k,l) -Euler theorem and the combinatorics of (k,l) -sequences
Isaac KONAN

Abstract
. In 1997, Bousquet-Mélou and Eriksson stated a broad generalization of Euler’s distinct-odd partition theorem, namely the (k,l) -Euler theorem. Their identity involved the (k,l) -lecture-hall partitions, which, unlike usual difference conditions of partitions in Rogers-Ramanujan type identities, satisfy some ratio constraints. In a 2008 paper, in response to a question suggested by Richard Stanley, Savage and Yee provided a simple bijection for the l -lecture-hall partitions (the case k=l ), whose specialization in l=2 corresponds to Sylvester’s bijection. Subsequently, as an open question, a generalization of their bijection was suggested for the case k,l\geq 2 . In the spirit of Savage and Yee’s work, we provide and prove in this paper slight variations of the suggested bijection, not only for the case k,l\geq 2 , but also for the cases (k,1) and (1,k) with k\geq 4 . Furthermore, we show that our bijections equal the recursive bijections given by Bousquet-Mélou and Eriksson in their recursive proof of the (k,l) -lecture hall theorem and finally provide the analogous recursive bijection for the (k,l) -Euler theorem.



Contents

1.
Introduction

Let
\lambda
be a
finite
sequence
(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{t})
of non-negative integers. The integers
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{t}
,
t
and
\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{t}=|\lambda|
are respectively called the
parts
, the
length
and the
weight
of
\lambda
. By convention, for
\lambda=\emptyset
, the length and weight both equal 0.
Let
|\lambda|_{e},|\lambda|_{o}
be respectively the even and odd weights of
\lambda
, and such that

|\lambda|_{e}=\sum_{i\text{ even}}\lambda_{i}\qquad,\qquad|\lambda|_{o}=\sum_{i\text{ odd}}\lambda_{i}\,\,\cdot

Thus,
|\lambda|_{e}+|\lambda|_{o}=|\lambda|
. Conventionally, a
partition
of an integer
n
refers to such finite sequence
\lambda
with positive parts, occurring in a
non-increasing
order, and with weight
n
. The sequence
\emptyset
is then the unique partition of 0.

Examples 1.1
.

The sequence
(0,0,1,2,5,5)
has length 6, even weight 7 and odd weight 6, while the sequence
(0,1,2,5,5)
has length 5, even weight 6 and odd weight 7.

A major identity of the partition theory, Euler’s theorem, states that for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the sets of partitions of
n
into respectively distinct parts and odd parts are equinumerous. There have been number of generalizations of Euler’s theorem, mainly as identities dealing with partitions whose parts satisfied difference constraints
[
1
,
9
,
10
,
15
]
. Yet, in this paper, our discussion focuses on an intricate identity given by Bousquet-Mélou and Eriksson
[
2
]
, with successive parts constrained by a certain ratio.

Theorem 1.2

(Lecture hall theorem)
.

Let
n
be a positive integer, and denote by
\mathcal{L}_{n}
the set of partitions
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{t})
such that
t\leq n
and
(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{n+1-i})_{i=1}^{t}
is non-increasing. Then, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the number of partitions of
m
in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
is equal to the number of partitions of
m
into odd parts less than
2n
. The corresponding identity is

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{L}_{n}}q^{|\lambda|}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-q^{2i-1}}\,\cdot

Such partitions
\lambda
are referred to as lecture hall partitions.

The above theorem yields the Euler identity when
n
tends to
\infty
. Subsequently, Bousquet-Mélou and Eriksson discovered in
[
3
]
a generalization of Theorem
1.2
involving a particular sequence of integers named the
(k,l)
-sequence.
For
k,l
positive integers such that
kl\geq 4
, the
(k,l)
-sequence consists of the integers
(a^{(k,l)}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
recursively defined by

\begin{cases}a^{(k,l)}_{2n}=la^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n-2}\\
a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1}=ka^{(k,l)}_{2n}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}\end{cases}

(1.0.1)

for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, with the initial conditions
a^{(k,l)}_{i}=i
for
i\in\{0,1\}
. When
k=l
, the sequence is called an
l
-sequence, and we note
a_{n}^{(l)}
instead of
a_{n}^{(k,l)}
. By abuse of notation, in the remainder of the paper, when
k=l\geq 2
, we replace
(k,l)
by
(l)
. This convention will be helpful in the study of the cases
(k,1)
and
(1,k)
for
k\geq 4
.

(1)

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Denote by
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{n}
the set of non-negative integers sequences
\lambda
with length
n
, i.e.
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
, and such that

\frac{\lambda_{1}}{a^{(k,l)}_{1}}\leq\frac{\lambda_{2}}{a^{(k,l)}_{2}}\leq\cdots\leq\frac{\lambda_{n-1}}{a^{(k,l)}_{n-1}}\leq\frac{\lambda_{n}}{a^{(k,l)}_{n}}\,\cdot

(1.0.2)

The sequences
\lambda
are called the
(k,l)
-lecture hall partitions
.

(2)

For
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 4}
, let
u_{m}
be the greatest radical of the equation

0=x^{2}-\sqrt{m}\cdot x+1\,\cdot

Denote by
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}
the set of sequences
\lambda
with a positive even number of parts, i.e.
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2t})
, and such that
0=\lambda_{2t}\leq\lambda_{2t-1}
, and for
1\leq i\leq t-1
,

\sqrt{\frac{k}{l}}u_{kl}\cdot\lambda_{2i+1}<\lambda_{2i}<\sqrt{\frac{k}{l}}u^{-1}_{kl}\lambda_{2i-1}\,\cdot

(1.0.3)

The set
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}
defines the set of
(k,l)
-Euler partitions
.

(3)

Define
b^{(k,l)}_{i}=a^{(k,l)}_{i}+a^{(l,k)}_{i-1}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}_{n}
be the set of finite sequences
\lambda=(b^{(k,l)}_{i_{1}},\ldots,b^{(k,l)}_{i_{t}})
such that
1\leq i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{t}\leq n
, and set

\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}_{n}\,\cdot

Note that the sequences in
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}
are determined by the number of occurrences of each
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
. To ease the notation, we denote by

\displaystyle\prod_{i\geq 1}\left(b^{(k,l)}_{i}\right)^{m_{i}}

the sequence of
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}
with
m_{i}
occurrences of
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
for
i\geq 1
.

Remark 1.3
.

When
k=l=2
,
a^{(k,l)}_{n}=n
and
b^{(k,l)}_{n}=2n-1
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence,
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}_{n}
corresponds to the set of partitions into odd parts less than
2n
, and
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}
is to the set of partitions into odd parts. Furthermore,
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{n}
can be identified to
\mathcal{L}_{n}
. In fact, for
\lambda=(\lambda_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{n}
with
t
positive parts, i.e
\lambda_{n-t}=0<\lambda_{n+1-t}
, it suffices to consider the partition
\mu=(\mu_{i})_{i=1}^{t}=(\lambda_{n+1-i})_{i=1}^{t}
which, by definition, belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{n}
. Inversely, for
\mu=(\mu_{i})_{i=1}^{t}\in\mathcal{L}_{n}
, set
\lambda_{i}=0
for
1\leq i\leq n-t
, and
\lambda_{i}=\mu_{n+1-i}
for
n+1-t\leq i\leq n
, and one can check that
\lambda=(\lambda_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{n}
. Finally, as
\sqrt{\frac{k}{l}}u_{kl}=\sqrt{\frac{k}{l}}u_{kl}^{-1}=1
for
k=l=2
,
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}
then corresponds to the set of partitions into distinct parts. From any partition into distinct parts, the corresponding Euler partition is obtained by adding one or two parts equal to 0 according to whether its length is odd or even.

The Bousquet-Mélou–Eriksson generalization of Theorem
1.2
, denoted the
(k,l)
-lecture hall theorem
, is the following.

Theorem 1.4

(The
(k,l)
-lecture hall theorem)
.

Let
k,l,n
be positive integers such that
kl\geq 4
. Then,

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{2n}}x^{|\lambda|_{o}}y^{|\lambda|_{e}}=\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\frac{1}{1-x^{a^{(l,k)}_{i-1}}y^{a^{(k,l)}_{i}}}\,,

(1.0.4)

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}}x^{|\lambda|_{o}}y^{|\lambda|_{e}}=\prod_{i=1}^{2n-1}\frac{1}{1-x^{a^{(l,k)}_{i}}y^{a^{(k,l)}_{i-1}}}\,\cdot

(1.0.5)

This implies that, for fixed weight
m
in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there are as many
(k,l)
-lecture hall partitions in
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{2n}
as sequences in
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}_{2n}
,
and there are as many
(k,l)
-lecture hall partitions in
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}
as sequences in
\mathcal{B}^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}
.

We recover the lecture hall theorem with the transformation
(k,l,x,y)\mapsto(2,2,q,q)
. As Euler’s theorem is implied by Theorem
1.2
with
n\rightarrow\infty
,
a suitable generalization can then be deduced from (
1.0.4
) using the same approach.

Theorem 1.5

(The
(k,l)
-Euler theorem)
.

Let
k,l
be positive integers such that
kl\geq 4
. Then,

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}}x^{|\lambda|_{o}}y^{|\lambda|_{e}}=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1-x^{a^{(k,l)}_{i}}y^{a^{(l,k)}_{i-1}}}\,\cdot

(1.0.6)

This implies that, for fixed weight
m
in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, there are as many
(k,l)
-Euler partitions in
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}
as sequences in
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}
.

Many variations of the lecture hall theorem can be found in the literature
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
]
, and allow a deeper comprehension of sequences constrained by ratio conditions. Lecture hall partitions have raised interest not only in combinatorics, but also in number theory, algebra and geometry, and allow new interpretations and generalizations of classical theorems.

Our matter in this paper concerns the combinatorial aspects of these structures. One of the most effective way to uncover the combinatorial structures of mathematical objects consists in constructing bijections. In their proof of Theorem
1.4
, Bousquet-Mélou and Eriksson used generating functions satisfying some recursive relations, recursion which indeed gives rise to a recursive bijection. However, the quest of finding a simple bijective proof remained unsolved until the work of Savage and Yee
[
12
]
.

In the spirit of a bijective proof of Theorem
1.2
given by Yee
[
14
]
, they provided simple bijections for the
l
-lecture hall and
l
-Euler theorems, which are the restrictions of respectively Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
for the case
k=l\geq 2
. They introduced for that purpose two novel tools, the Sylvester diagrams and
a^{(l)}
-interpretations of partitions. The method has a great connection to the Sylvester bijection for Euler’s theorem
[
13
]
, which is recovered in the case
k=l=2
of their bijection. They also suggested, without proof, a generalization of their method for the case
k,l\geq 2
.

The goal of this paper is to prove the well-definedness of the suggested bijection for
k,l\geq 2
, and also construct the suitable analogous bijection for the cases
(k,1)
and
(1,k)
with
k\geq 4
. In addition, in response to open question of Savage in
[
11
]
, we prove that the bijections for the
(k,l)
-lecture hall theorem are equivalent to the Bousquet-Mélou–Eriksson recursive bijection, and provide the analogous recurvise bijection for the
(k,l)
-Euler theorem. Our argumentation dwells on a revisited
a^{(k,l)}
-interpretation of partitions. The case
k,l\geq 2
is closely related the
a^{(l)}
-interpretation of Savage and Yee, and can be viewed as such but presenting in a more formalized way. The novelty of our work indeed consists in adapting the formal method to deal with the cases
(k,1)
and
(1,k)
, and for that purpose, the main trick is to view certain parts, not as integers, but as pairs of integers satisfying simple arithmetic conditions.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we present some fundamental properties of the
(k,l)
-sequence and some tools and lemmas that will be useful to comprehend the mechanics of the bijections.
Section
3
is dedicated to the description of our bijections for Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.4
. Then, in Section
4
, we investigate the combinatorics behind the structure of
(k,l)
-sequence through the
(k,l)
-admissible words, and provide in these terms interpretations of lecture hall and Euler partitions. In Sections
5
,
6
and
7
, we prove the well-definedness of our bijections respectively for Theorem
1.5
, and Theorem
1.4
in the even and odd cases.
Finally, we show in Section
8
the equivalence between our bijections and the recursive bijection, construct the analogous recursive bijection for the
(k,l)
-Euler theorem and prove its equivalence to our bijections.

For the convenience of the reader, we postpone the proof of technical lemmas and propositions to the appendices.

2.
Preliminary

In this section, we outline key properties of the
(k,l)
-sequence, and provide simple tools to deal with the ratio constraints of our partitions.

2.1.
Properties of
(k,l)
-sequences

Let
k,l
be positive integers such that
kl\geq 4
. Recall that
u_{m}
is the greatest radical of the equation
x^{2}+1=\sqrt{m}\cdot x
. Then,
u_{kl}=\frac{\sqrt{kl}+\sqrt{kl-4}}{2}
,
u_{kl}^{-1}=\frac{\sqrt{kl}-\sqrt{kl-4}}{2}
, and one can check that, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\begin{cases}a^{(k,l)}_{2n}=\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{l}{k}}\cdot\frac{u_{kl}^{2n}-u_{kl}^{-2n}}{u_{kl}-u_{kl}^{-1}}\\
\\
a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1}=\displaystyle\frac{u_{kl}^{2n+1}-u_{kl}^{-2n-1}}{u_{kl}-u_{kl}^{-1}}\end{cases}\cdot

(2.1.1)

Note that both expressions can be indeed written as polynomials in
u_{kl},u_{kl}^{-1}
, so that, in terms of limit, they
still
hold for
u_{kl}=1
, i.e. ,
kl=4
. We also observe that, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a^{(k,l)}_{-n}=a^{(k,l)}_{n}
. Moreover, since
u_{m}^{2}=u_{(m-2)^{2}}
, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\begin{cases}a^{(k,l)}_{2n}=la_{n}^{(kl-2)}\,,\\
a^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}=a_{n}^{(kl-2)}+a_{n-1}^{(kl-2)}\end{cases}\,,

(2.1.2)

so that
a^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}=a^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}
and
ka^{(k,l)}_{2n}=la^{(l,k)}_{2n}
. Furthermore,
the sequences
(a^{(k,l)}_{2n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1})_{n\geq 0}
are increasing. By (
2.1.1
), for
n\geq 1

\displaystyle a^{(k,l)}_{2n}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n-2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{l}{k}}(u^{2n-1}+u^{-2n+1})\,,

\displaystyle a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}

\displaystyle=u^{2n}+u^{-2n}

so that both differences are positive. In particular, for
k,l\geq 2
,
(a^{(k,l)}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is increasing, as

\displaystyle a^{(k,l)}_{2n}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{2}(a^{(k,l)}_{2n}+a^{(k,l)}_{2n-2})=\frac{l}{2}a^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}\,,

\displaystyle a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{2}(a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1}+a^{(k,l)}_{2n-1})=\frac{k}{2}a^{(k,l)}_{2n}\,\cdot

Finally,for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set
s^{(k,l)}_{2n+1}=u_{kl}^{-2n}
and
s^{(k,l)}_{2n}=\sqrt{l/k}\cdot u^{-2n+1}_{kl}
. One can check that
(s^{(k,l)}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies the recursive relation (
1.0.1
), and the inequalities (
1.0.3
) in the
(k,l)
-Euler partitions become

\begin{cases}\lambda_{2i-1}>s^{(k,l)}_{0}\cdot\lambda_{2i}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq t-1\,,\\
\lambda_{2i}>s^{(l,k)}_{0}\cdot\lambda_{2i+1}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq t-1\,\cdot\end{cases}

(2.1.3)

Observe that
(s^{(l,k)}_{0})^{-1}=s^{(k,l)}_{2}
, and since
u_{kl}\geq 1
, the sequences
(s^{(k,l)}_{2n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(s^{(k,l)}_{2n-1})_{n\geq 1}
are non-increasing. In particular, when
k,l\geq 2
,
(s^{(k,l)}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is non-increasing. Furthermore, for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
s^{(k,l)}_{2n}=s^{(k,l)}_{2}s_{n}^{(kl-2)}
and
s^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}=s_{n}^{(kl-2)}

Examples 2.1
.

(1)

For
(k,l)=(2,2)
,
a_{n}^{(2,2)}=a_{n}^{(2)}=n
and
s_{n}^{(2,2)}=s_{n}^{(2)}=1
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

For
(k,l)=(4,1)
,
a_{2n}^{(1,4)}=4a_{2n}^{(4,1)}=4n
, and
a_{2n-1}^{(4,1)}=2n-1
, while
s_{2n}^{(1,4)}=4s_{2n}^{(4,1)}=2
and
s_{2n-1}^{(4,1)}=1
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

For
(k,l)=(5,1)
,
u_{5}
equals the golden number
\phi=\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
, and
a_{2n}^{(1,5)}=5a_{2n}^{(5,1)}=5F_{2n}
, and
a_{2n-1}^{(5,1)}=F_{2n}+F_{2n-2}
, where
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the Fibonacci sequence, satisfying
(F_{0},F_{1})=(0,1)
and
F_{n+2}=F_{n+1}+F_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,
s_{2n}^{(1,5)}=5s_{2n}^{(5,1)}=\sqrt{5}\phi^{1-2n}
and
s_{2n+1}^{(5,1)}=\phi^{-2n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We now state in the following proposition the main properties satisfied by the
(k,l)
-sequences, and which are fundamental for the study of the ratio constraints.

Proposition 2.2
.

The following properties hold.

(1)

For
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle a^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}a^{(k,l)}_{m}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n}a^{(l,k)}_{m-1}

\displaystyle=a^{(k,l)}_{2n-m}\,,

(2.1.4)

\displaystyle a^{(k,l)}_{2n}a^{(l,k)}_{m+1}-a^{(l,k)}_{2n+1}a^{(k,l)}_{m}

\displaystyle=a^{(k,l)}_{2n-m}\,\cdot

(2.1.5)

(2)

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle a^{(k,l)}_{n}-s^{(k,l)}_{0}\cdot a^{(l,k)}_{n-1}

\displaystyle=s^{(k,l)}_{n}

(2.1.6)

Hence, the sequence
(a^{(k,l)}_{n+1}/a^{(l,k)}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is decreasing and tends to
s^{(k,l)}_{0}
.

2.2.
Tools and notations

In the remainder of this paper,
\chi(prop)
equals 1 if
prop
is true and 0 if not, and for
i,n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\delta_{i,n}=\chi(i=n)
. The notation
\mathbb{R}
denotes the set of real numbers, and for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\lfloor x\rfloor
denotes the greatest integer smaller or equal to
x
, i.e.

\lfloor x\rfloor\leq x<\lfloor x\rfloor+1\,,

and
\lceil x\rceil
refers to the smallest integer greater or equal to
x
, i.e.

\lceil x\rceil-1<x\leq\lceil x\rceil\,\cdot

Remark 2.3
.

For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, if
x+y\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
x+y=\lceil x\rceil+\lfloor y\rfloor
, as

x+y-1<\lceil x\rceil+\lfloor y\rfloor<x+y+1\,\cdot

Remark 2.4
.

For
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

\displaystyle r>as

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow r\geq\lfloor as\rfloor+1\Longleftrightarrow\lceil r/a\rceil-1\geq s\,,

\displaystyle r\geq as

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow r\geq\lceil as\rceil\Longleftrightarrow\lfloor r/a\rfloor\geq s\,\cdot

Remark
2.4
yields a more accurate inequality between the parts involved in
(k,l)
-lecture hall and
(k,l)
-Euler partitions.

2.2.1.
Divide and conquer

The key lemma playing a fundamental role in the combinatorics of
(k,1)
and
(1,k)
sequences is the following.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}\leq x<1
be a real number.

(1)

Set
S_{x}^{+}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}:0<(1-x)a-xb\leq 1\}
.
Then,

\displaystyle S_{x}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}:1+\left\lfloor\frac{xb}{1-x}\right\rfloor\leq a\leq 1+\left\lfloor\frac{x(b+1)}{1-x}\right\rfloor\right\}\cdot

(2.2.1)

In particular,

x<(1-x)a-xb\leq 1\Longleftrightarrow a=1+\left\lfloor\frac{x(b+1)}{1-x}\right\rfloor\,\cdot

(2.2.2)

Moreover, for
(a,b)\in S_{x}^{+}
,

(a)

(a+1,b)\in S_{x}^{+}
if and only if
1+\left\lfloor\frac{xb}{1-x}\right\rfloor\leq a<1+\left\lfloor\frac{x(b+1)}{1-x}\right\rfloor
,

(b)

and
(a,b+1)\in S_{x}^{+}
if and only if
a=1+\left\lfloor\frac{x(b+1)}{1-x}\right\rfloor
.

Hence, the function
(a,b)\mapsto a+b
defines a bijection from
S_{x}^{+}
to
\mathbb{Z}
, whose inverse is defined by
m\mapsto(\lfloor xm\rfloor+1,\lceil(1-x)m\rceil-1)
.

(2)

Set
S_{x}^{-}=\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}:0\leq(1-x)a-xb<1\}
. Then,

\displaystyle S_{x}^{-}

\displaystyle=\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}:\left\lceil\frac{xb}{1-x}\right\rceil\leq a\leq\left\lceil\frac{x(b+1)}{1-x}\right\rceil\right\}\,\cdot

(2.2.3)

In particular,

x\leq(1-x)a-xb<1\Longleftrightarrow a=\left\lceil\frac{x(b+1)}{1-x}\right\rceil\,\cdot

(2.2.4)

Moreover, for
(a,b)\in S_{x}^{-}
,

(a)

(a+1,b)\in S_{x}^{-}
if and only if
\left\lceil\frac{xb}{1-x}\right\rceil\leq a<\left\lceil\frac{x(b+1)}{1-x}\right\rceil
,

(b)

and
(a,b+1)\in S_{x}^{-}
if and only if
a=\left\lceil\frac{x(b+1)}{1-x}\right\rceil
.

Hence, the function
(a,b)\mapsto a+b
defines a bijection from
from
S_{x}^{-}
to
\mathbb{Z}
, whose inverse is defined by
m\mapsto(\lceil xm\rceil,\lfloor(1-x)m\rfloor)
.

For a pair of a element
s=(s_{1},s_{2})
, we set
p_{i}(s)=s_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Note that the map
m\mapsto(\lfloor xm\rfloor+1,\lceil(1-x)m\rceil-1)
induces a bijection from
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
to
\{s\in S_{x}^{+}:p_{2}(s)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
. Splitting a positive integer into such a unique pair will be the main trick that eases the study of
(k,1)
and
(1,k)
-Euler partitions. Similarly, the map
m\mapsto(\lceil xm\rceil,\lfloor(1-x)m\rfloor)
induces a bijection from
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
to
\{s\in S_{x}^{-}:p_{2}(s)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}
, and this splitting will ease the study of the
(k,1)
and
(1,k)
lecture hall partitions.

2.2.2.
Succession in an ordered set

Let
(S,\preceq)
be a countable and total ordered set. Recall that
c\prec d
means that
c\preceq d
and
c\neq d
. We say that
c
and
d
are consecutive in
S
if and only if
c\prec d
, but there is no
e\in S
such that
c\prec e\prec d
. For such element
c
and
d
,
c

precedes

d
and
d

follows

c
in
S
.
In general, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
c
is the
m^{th}
element that
precedes

d
in
S
or
d
is the
m^{th}
element that
follows

c
in
S
, if and only if there exists
s_{0},\ldots,s_{m}\in S
such that
s_{0}=c
,
s_{m}=d
and for
1\leq i\leq m
,
s_{i-1}
precedes
s_{i}
in
S
. We note

d=\mathcal{F}(m,S,c)\,\cdot

(2.2.5)

Remark 2.6
.

Observe that
c=\mathcal{F}(0,S,c)
, and
d=\mathcal{F}(1,S,c)
is the element that follows
c
in
S
.

The last tool of this part is presented in the following lemma and will help to prove the well-definedness of our bijections.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
(S,\preceq)
be an infinite, countable and total ordered set with a minimal element. Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
c_{1},c_{2},d_{1},d_{2}\in S
such that
d_{i}=\mathcal{F}(m,S,c_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Then,
c_{1}\preceq c_{2}
if and only if
d_{1}\preceq d_{2}
. Moreover, if
d_{1}\preceq d_{2}
and
d_{1}
admits an
m^{th}
element
c_{1}
that precedes it, then
d_{2}
admits an
m^{th}
element
c_{2}
that precedes it and we have
c_{1}\preceq c_{2}
.

3.
Bijections

This part is dedicated to the presentation of the bijection for Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
.
Denote by
\Phi^{(k,l)}
the bijection from
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}
to
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, denote by
\Phi_{2n}^{(k,l)}
the bijection from
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}_{2n}
to
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{2n}
and by
\Phi_{2n-1}^{(k,l)}
the bijection from
\mathcal{B}^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}
to
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}
.
In the remainder of the paper, we illustrate our bijections and key definitions with
kl=6
, i.e. with
(k,l)\in\{(2,3),(3,2),(6,1),(1,6)\}
.

3.1.
The map
\Phi^{(k,l)}

We first describe the map
\Phi^{(k,l)}
. Let
\nu
be a partition in
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}
, and set
\lambda=(\lambda_{i})_{i\geq 1}
an infinite sequence of terms all equal to 0. We here proceed by
inserting
the parts
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
into the pairs
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
, starting from the smallest
j
and the greatest
i
.

3.1.1.
The case
k,l\geq 2

(1)

To insert
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
with
i>1
into
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
, proceed as follows.
If

\lambda_{2j-1}-s^{(k,l)}_{0}\cdot\lambda_{2j}>s^{(k,l)}_{i-1}-s^{(k,l)}_{i}\,,

(3.1.1)

then do

(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,l)}_{i}-a^{(k,l)}_{i-1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(l,k)}_{i-1}-a^{(l,k)}_{i-2})

(3.1.2)

and store
b^{(k,l)}_{i-1}
for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
. This action is equivalent to inserting one part
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
by adding
b^{(k,l)}_{i}-b^{(k,l)}_{i-1}
to the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
and storing one part
b^{(k,l)}_{i-1}
for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
. Else, we do

(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,l)}_{i},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(l,k)}_{i-1})\,\cdot

(3.1.3)

This action means that we insert one part
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
by adding it to the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
.

(2)

To insert
b^{(k,l)}_{1}
, do (
3.1.3
) for
i=1
.

3.1.2.
The case
(k,1)

(1)

To insert the parts
b^{(k,1)}_{i}
with
i>2
into the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
, proceed as follows.

(a)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
for
i>1
, if

\lambda_{2j-1}-s^{(k,1)}_{0}\cdot\lambda_{2j}>s^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}-s^{(k,1)}_{2i}\,,

(3.1.4)

then do

(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2i-2},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2i-3})

(3.1.5)

and store, for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
,
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
if there are at least two remaining parts
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
to insert, and
b^{(k,l)}_{2i-2}
if there is only one remaining part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
. The first action consists in inserting two parts
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
by adding

b^{(k,1)}_{2i}-b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}=2b^{(k,1)}_{2i}-b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
to the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
, and storing
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
, while the second action, which can occur once, consists in inserting the last remaining part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
by adding
to the pair
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}-b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
, and storing one part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
.
If condition (
3.1.4
) is not satisfied, do

(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1})\,\cdot

(3.1.6)

This implies that we insert one part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
by adding it to the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
.

(b)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
for
i>1
, if
(
3.1.4
) occurs, then do (
3.1.5
)
and store for the the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
two parts
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
. This action consists in inserting one part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
by adding
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}-b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}=b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}-2b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
to the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
, and storing two parts
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
. Else, do (
3.1.6
)
and store for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
one part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
.
This action consists in inserting one part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
by adding
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
to the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
, and storing one part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}=b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}-b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
.

(2)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2}
, if

\lambda_{2j-1}-s^{(k,1)}_{0}\cdot\lambda_{2j}>s^{(k,1)}_{0}\,,

(3.1.7)

then do

(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2}-a^{(k,1)}_{1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{1}-a^{(1,k)}_{0})\,,

(3.1.8)

and store for the the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
one part
b^{(k,1)}_{1}
. Else, do (
3.1.6
) with
i=1
.

(3)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{1}
, do

(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{0})\,\cdot

(3.1.9)

3.1.3.
The case
(1,k)

(1)

To insert the parts
b^{(k,1)}_{i}
with
i>1
into the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
, proceed as follows.

(a)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
for
i\geq 1
, if

\lambda_{2j-1}-s^{(1,k)}_{0}\cdot\lambda_{2j}>s^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}-s^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}\,,

(3.1.10)

then do

(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2i-2})

(3.1.11)

and store for the the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
,
b^{(1,k)}_{2i}
if there are at least two remaining parts
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
to insert, and
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
if there is only one remaining part
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
. Else, do

(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i+1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i})\,\cdot

(3.1.12)

(b)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{2i}
for
i\geq 1
, if (
3.1.10
) occurs
then do (
3.1.11
)
and store for the the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
two parts
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
. Else, do (
3.1.12
)
and store for the the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j+2})
one part
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
.

(2)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{1}
, do (
3.1.12
) for
i=0
.

Whatever the case, we stop at
(\lambda_{2t-1},\lambda_{2t})
when there is no part
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
to insert for
i\geq 2
. Observe that the part size of
\lambda_{2j}
increases after an insertion of
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
if and only if
i>1
, and there is no store part after the insertion of
b^{(k,l)}_{1}
. Thus,
t
is the smallest integer
j
such that
\lambda_{2j}=0
after all the insertions, and
\lambda_{2t-1}
is then equal to the number of inserted
b^{(k,l)}_{1}
into the pair
(\lambda_{2t-1},\lambda_{2t})
. We finally set

\Phi^{(k,l)}(\nu)=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2t})
.

Observe that the map
\Phi^{(k,l)}
is weight-preserving, and that the
a^{(k,l)}_{i}
-component and
a^{(l,k)}_{i-1}
-component of the parts
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
only contribute respectively to the odd and the even weights of the image.

Remark 3.1
.

By (
2.1.6
), the following equivalences occur:

(
3.1.1
)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,l)}_{i}-a^{(k,l)}_{i-1}>s^{(k,l)}_{0}(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(l,k)}_{i-1}-a^{(l,k)}_{i-2})\,,

(
3.1.4
)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}>s^{(k,1)}_{0}(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2i-3})\,,

(
3.1.7
)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2}-a^{(k,1)}_{1}>s^{(k,1)}_{0}(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{1}-a^{(1,k)}_{0})\,,

(
3.1.10
)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}>s^{(1,k)}_{0}(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2i-2})\,\cdot

In practice, we conveniently apply these equivalences to compute the image by
\Phi^{(k,l)}
. Our formulations only aim at easing the calculations in the proof of the well-definedness. Moreover, the case
k=l
of the first equivalence yields to the condition of the bijection given by Savage and Yee for the
l
-Euleur Theorem. Note that in the case of
k,l\geq 2
, with the order of the insertions, starting from the greatest available part
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
, we can equivalently insert
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
in
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, then insert the stored part
b^{(k,1)}_{i-1}
in
(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4})
, and so on. For this reason, our map
\Phi^{(l,l)}
matches Savage and Yee’s bijection.

(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4})
, and only apply (
3.1.6
) for
i=2,1
to obtain
(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4})=(19,22)
, and store
b_{4}^{(6,1)}
for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{5},\lambda_{6})
. Then, for the pair
(\lambda_{5},\lambda_{6})
, we do once (
3.1.6
) with
i=2
and obtain
(\lambda_{5},\lambda_{6})=(4,5)
. We finally set
\Phi^{(6,1)}(\nu)=(81,100,19,22,4,5,0,0)\in\mathcal{L}^{(6,1)}
.

(4)

The case
(1,6)
with
\nu=(b_{1}^{(1,6)})^{2}(b_{2}^{(1,6)})^{5}(b_{3}^{(1,6)})^{2}(b_{4}^{(1,6)})^{3}(b_{6}^{(1,6)})^{5}=(1+0)^{2}(6+1)^{5}(5+1)^{2}(24+5)^{3}(90+19)^{5}

b_{5}^{(1,6)}
. Finally, we apply once (

) and obtain

and store
b_{5}^{(1,6)}
.

) to obtain

to obtain

.

3.2.
The map
\Phi_{2n}^{(k,l)}

We now describe the bijection
\Phi_{2n}^{(k,l)}
. Let
\nu
be a partition in
\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)}_{2n}
, and set
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2n})
, all parts equal to 0. As in the previous construction, we here proceed by step according to the insertion of the parts
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
into the pairs
(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})
, starting from the greatest
i,j
.

3.2.1.
The case
k,l\geq 2

(1)

To insert
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
with
i>1
in
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
, proceed as follows.
If

a^{(k,l)}_{2j-1}\lambda_{2j}-a^{(k,l)}_{2j}\lambda_{2j-1}\geq a^{(k,l)}_{2j-i+1}-a^{(k,l)}_{2j-i}\,\,,

(3.2.1)

then do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,l)}_{i}-a^{(k,l)}_{i-1},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(l,k)}_{i-1}-a^{(l,k)}_{i-2})

(3.2.2)

and store
b^{(l,k)}_{i-1}
for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j-2},\lambda_{2j-1})
. Else, do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,l)}_{i},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(l,k)}_{i-1})\,\cdot

(3.2.3)

(2)

To insert
b^{(k,l)}_{1}
, do (
3.2.3
) for
i=1
.

3.2.2.
The case
(k,1)

(1)

To insert the parts
b^{(k,1)}_{i}
with
i>2
into the pair
(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})
, proceed as follows.

(a)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
for
i>1
, if

a^{(k,1)}_{2j-1}\lambda_{2j}-a^{(k,1)}_{2j}\lambda_{2j-1}\geq a^{(k,1)}_{2j+2-2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2j-2i}\,,

(3.2.4)

then do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2i-2},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2i-3})

(3.2.5)

and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-2},\lambda_{2j-3})
,
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
if there are at least two remaining parts
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
to insert, and
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
if there is only one remaining part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
. Else, do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1})\,\cdot

(3.2.6)

(b)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
for
i>1
, if (
3.2.4
) occurs, then do (
3.2.5
)
and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-2},\lambda_{2j-3})
two parts
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
. Else, do (
3.2.6
)
and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-2},\lambda_{2j-3})
one part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
.

(2)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2}
, if

a^{(k,1)}_{2j-1}\lambda_{2j}-a^{(k,1)}_{2j}\lambda_{2j-1}\geq a^{(k,1)}_{2j}

(3.2.7)

then do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{2}-a^{(k,1)}_{1},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(1,k)}_{1}-a^{(1,k)}_{0})

(3.2.8)

and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-2},\lambda_{2j-3})
one part
b^{(k,1)}_{1}
. Else, do (
3.2.6
) for
i=1
.

(3)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{1}
, do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{1},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(1,k)}_{0})\,\cdot

(3.2.9)

3.2.3.
The case
(1,k)

(1)

To insert the parts
b^{(1,k)}_{i}
with
2j\neq i>1
into the pair
(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})
, proceed as follows.

(a)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
for
i\geq 1
, if

a^{(1,k)}_{2j-1}\lambda_{2j}-a^{(1,k)}_{2j}\lambda_{2j-1}\geq a^{(1,k)}_{2j+1-2i}-a^{(1,k)}_{2j-1-2i}\,,

(3.2.10)

then do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2i-2})

(3.2.11)

and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-2},\lambda_{2j-3})
,
b^{(1,k)}_{2i}
if there are at least two remaining parts
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
to insert, and
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
if there is only one remaining part
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
. Else, do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i+1},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i})\,\cdot

(3.2.12)

(b)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{2i}
for
j\neq i\geq 1
, if (
3.2.10
) occurs,
then do (
3.2.11
)
and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-2},\lambda_{2j-3})
two parts
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
. Else, do (
3.2.12
)
and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-2},\lambda_{2j-3})
one part
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
.

(2)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2j\}
, do

(\lambda_{2j},\lambda_{2j-1})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{i},\,\lambda_{2j-1}+a^{(k,1)}_{i-1})\,\cdot

(3.2.13)

Similarly to
\Phi^{(k,l)}
, the map
\Phi^{(k,l)}
is weight-preserving, and the
a^{(k,l)}_{i}
-component and
a^{(l,k)}_{i-1}
-component of the parts
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
only contribute respectively to the even and the odd weights of the image.

Examples 3.3
.

, with

3.3.
The map
\Phi_{2n-1}^{(k,l)}

We finally describe the bijection
\Phi_{2n-1}^{(k,l)}
. Let
\nu
be a partition in
\mathcal{B}^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}
, and set
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2n-1})
, all parts equal to 0. We again proceed by inserting the parts
b^{(l,k)}_{i}
into the pairs
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})
with
0\leq j<n
, starting from the greatest
i,j
. We here consider a fictitious part
\lambda_{0}=0
.

3.3.1.
The case
k,l\geq 2

(1)

To insert
b^{(l,k)}_{i}
with
i>1
in
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})
, proceed as follows.
If

a^{(k,l)}_{2j}\lambda_{2j+1}-a^{(k,l)}_{2j+1}\lambda_{2j}\geq a^{(k,l)}_{2j+2-i}-a^{(k,l)}_{2j+1-i}\,\,,

(3.3.1)

then do

(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j+1}+a^{(l,k)}_{i}-a^{(l,k)}_{i-1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,l)}_{i-1}-a^{(k,l)}_{i-2})

(3.3.2)

and store
b^{(l,k)}_{i-1}
for the insertion into the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j-2})
. Else, do

(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j+1}+a^{(l,k)}_{i},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,l)}_{i-1})\,\cdot

(3.3.3)

(2)

To insert
b^{(k,l)}_{1}
, do (
3.3.3
) for
i=1
.

3.3.2.
The case
(k,1)

(1)

To insert the parts
b^{(1,k)}_{i}
with
i>1
into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})
, proceed as follows.

(a)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
for
i\geq 1
, if

a^{(k,1)}_{2j}\lambda_{2j+1}-a^{(k,1)}_{2j+1}\lambda_{2j}\geq a^{(k,1)}_{2j+2-2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2j-2i}\,,

(3.3.4)

then do

(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j+1}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2i-2})

(3.3.5)

and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j-2})
,
b^{(1,k)}_{2i}
if there are at least two remaining parts
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
to insert, and
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
if there is only one remaining part
b^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}
. Else, do

(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j+1}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i+1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i})\,\cdot

(3.3.6)

(b)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{2i}
for
i\geq 1
, if
(
3.3.4
) occurs,
then do
(
3.3.5
)
and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j-2})
two parts
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
. Else, do
(
3.3.6
)
and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j-2})
one part
b^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}
.

(2)

To insert
b^{(1,k)}_{1}
, do (
3.3.6
) for
i=0
.

3.3.3.
The case
(1,k)

(1)

To insert the parts
b^{(k,1)}_{i}
with
2j+1\neq i>2
into the pair
(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})
, proceed as follows.

(a)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
for
i>1
, if

a^{(1,k)}_{2j}\lambda_{2j+1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2j+1}\lambda_{2j}\geq a^{(1,k)}_{2j+3-2i}-a^{(1,k)}_{2j+1-2i}\,,

(3.3.7)

then do

(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j+1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i}-a^{(k,1)}_{2i-2},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}-a^{(1,k)}_{2i-3})

(3.3.8)

and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j-2})
,
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
if there are at least two remaining parts
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
to insert, and
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
if there is only one remaining part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i}
. Else, do

(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j+1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2i},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{2i-1})\,\cdot

(3.3.9)

(b)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}
for
j+1\neq i>1
, if
(
3.3.7
),
then do (
3.3.8
)
and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j-2})
two parts
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
. Else, do
(
3.3.9
)
and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j-2})
one part
b^{(k,1)}_{2i-2}
.

(2)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{2}
, if

a^{(k,1)}_{2j}\lambda_{2j+1}-a^{(k,1)}_{2j+1}\lambda_{2j}\geq a^{(k,1)}_{2j+1}

(3.3.10)

then do

(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j+1}+a^{(k,1)}_{2}-a^{(k,1)}_{1},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{1}-a^{(1,k)}_{0})\,,

(3.3.11)

and store for the insertion in the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j-2})
one part
b^{(k,1)}_{1}
. Else, do (
3.3.9
) for
i=1
.

(3)

To insert
b^{(k,1)}_{i}
for
i\in\{2j+1,1\}
, do

(\lambda_{2j+1},\lambda_{2j})\mapsto(\lambda_{2j+1}+a^{(k,1)}_{i},\,\lambda_{2j}+a^{(1,k)}_{i-1})\,\cdot

(3.3.12)

The map is weight-preserving, and the
a^{(l,k)}_{i}
-component and
a^{(k,l)}_{i-1}
-component of the parts
b^{(l,k)}_{i}
only contribute respectively to the odd and the even weights of the image.

4.
Combinatorics of
(k,l)
-admissible words

In this section, we investigate a system of numeration related to the
(k,l)
-sequence, namely the
(k,l)
-admissible words. This is a generalization of the
l
-admissible words used by Savage and Yee in
[
12
]
, which is a numeration system of non-negative integers presented by Frankeal in
[
8
]
.

4.1.
Definition of
(k,l)
-admissible words

4.1.1.
The case
k,l\geq 2

Let
k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
. Then,
(a^{(k,l)}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is an increasing sequence of positive integers. For conveniences, we define the sequence
(o^{(k,l)}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
such that
o^{(k,l)}_{2i-1}=l-2
and
o^{(k,l)}_{2i}=k-2
for
i\geq 1
.

Definition 4.1
.

An
infinite

(k,l)
-admissible word is a sequence
(c_{i})_{i\geq 1}
of non-negative integers such that :

(1)

there are finitely many positive terms,

(2)

c_{i}\in\{0,\ldots,o^{(k,l)}_{i}+1\}
,

(3)

there is no pair
1\leq i<j
such that

c_{h}=o^{(k,l)}_{h}+\chi(h\in\{i,j\})\qquad\text{for}\quad h\in\{i,i+1,\ldots,j\}\,\cdot

(4.1.1)

Denote
\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
by the set of all
(k,l)
-admissible words.
For
n\geq 1
, denote by
{}^{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
the set of all infinite
(k,l)
-admissible words such that
c_{i}=0
for
i\geq n
, and
{}_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
the set of all infinite
(k,l)
-admissible words such that
c_{i}=0
for
i<n
. Note that
(\,^{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)})_{n\geq 1}
is an increasing sequence of sets, while
(\,_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)})_{n\geq 1}
is decreasing. Set
{}^{n}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}=\,^{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}\cap\,_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
. Finally, for
s\in\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
, set the sequence
{}_{n}s
to be the sequence
s
where the
(n-1)
first terms are replaced by 0.

Example 4.2
.

In the case
(k,l)=(2,3)
,
(o^{(k,l)}_{2i-1},o^{(k,l)}_{2i})=(1,0)
for
i\geq 1
. Then,

(2,0,1,0,0,\ldots),(1,1,1,0,0,\ldots)\in\,^{4}\mathcal{C}^{(2,3)}
, but
(\underbrace{2,0,1,1},0,0,\ldots),(1,\underbrace{1,1,1},0,0,\ldots)\notin\mathcal{C}^{(2,3)}
, as the under-braced sub-sequences are forbidden. In the same way,
(\underbrace{1,1,1},0,0,\ldots)\notin\mathcal{C}^{(3,2)}
. We also have
{}_{1}(2,0,1,0,0,\ldots)=\,_{2}(2,0,1,0,0,\ldots)=(0,0,1,0,0,\ldots)
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\prec
be the lexicographic strict order on the set of integer sequences, defined by the following:

(c_{i})\prec(d_{i})\text{ if and only if there exists }n>0\text{ such that }c_{n}<d_{n}\text{ and }c_{i}=d_{i}\text{ for }{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i>n}\,\cdot

We also say that
(c_{i})\preceq(d_{i})
if and only if
(c_{i})=(d_{i})
or
(c_{i})\prec(d_{i})
,
(d_{i})\succ(c_{i})
if and only if
(c_{i})\prec(d_{i})
, and
(d_{i})\succeq(c_{i})
if and only if
(c_{i})\preceq(d_{i})
.

Let
\ll
be the lexicographic strict order on the set of integer sequences, defined by the following:

(c_{i})\ll(d_{i})\text{ if and only if there exists }n>0\text{ such that }c_{n}<d_{n}\text{ and }c_{i}=d_{i}\text{ for }{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i<n}\,\cdot

We say that
(d_{i})\gg(c_{i})
if and only if
(c_{i})\ll(d_{i})
.

As both
\prec
and
\ll
are both total orders, we can sort any set of integer sequences according either
\prec
or
\ll
.

Example 4.4
.

In
{}^{4}\mathcal{C}^{(2,3)}
,

(2,0,1,0,0,\ldots)\prec(1,1,1,0,0,\ldots)
, but
(2,0,1,0,0,\ldots)\gg(1,1,1,0,0,\ldots)
.

Remark 4.5
.

Observe that, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
(d_{i})_{i\geq 1}\in\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
,

_{n}(d_{i})_{i\geq 1}=(0,\ldots,0,d_{n},d_{n+1},\ldots)=\max\{(c_{i})_{i\geq 1}\in\,_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}:(c_{i})_{i\geq 1}\preceq(d_{i})_{i\geq 1}\}\,\cdot

(4.1.2)

The next lemma unveils a particular property of the forbidden sequences in
(k,l)
-admissible words that does not fit the lexicographic orders
\prec
and
\ll
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(w_{n}^{(k,l)})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence satisfying the same recursive relation as
(a^{(k,l)}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, i.e.

w_{n-1}^{(k,l)}+w_{n+1}^{(k,l)}=(o^{(k,l)}_{n}+2)\cdot w_{n}^{(k,l)}

(4.1.3)

Then, for
i\leq j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\sum_{h=i}^{j}o^{(k,l)}_{h}w_{h}^{(k,l)}=w_{i-1}^{(k,l)}+w_{j+1}^{(k,l)}-w_{i}^{(k,l)}-w_{j}^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

(4.1.4)

Hence, for
1\leq i<j
and
(c_{i},\ldots,c_{j})
a finite sequence satisfying (
4.1.1
),

\sum_{h=i}^{j}c_{h}w_{h}^{(k,l)}=w_{i-1}^{(k,l)}+w_{j+1}^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

(4.1.5)

In the following proposition, we present the interaction between the
(k,l)
-admissible words and the
(k,l)
-sequences through the lexicographic order, and show how that relation enables a numeration system of the non-negative integers in terms of the
(k,l)
-sequences.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
(w_{i}^{(k,l)})_{i\geq 0}
be a sequence of non-negative real numbers satisfying (
4.1.3
), and
define the function

\displaystyle\Gamma_{(k,l)}\colon\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle\ (c_{i})_{i\geq 1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i\geq 1}c_{i}w_{i}^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

(1)

When
(w_{i}^{(k,l)})_{i\geq 0}
is increasing,

(c_{i})\prec(d_{i})\Longleftrightarrow\Gamma_{(k,l)}((c_{i}))<\Gamma_{(k,l)}((d_{i}))\,,

(4.1.6)

and
\Gamma_{(k,l)}
is injective. Furthermore, if
(w_{i}^{(k,l)})_{i\geq 0}=(a^{(k,l)}_{i})_{i\geq 0}
,
\Gamma_{(k,l)}
then describes a bijection from
\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
which implies for
n\geq 1
a bijection from
{}^{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
to
\{0,\ldots,a^{(k,l)}_{n}-1\}
.
Moreover, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the inverse function, denoted by
\left[\cdot\right]^{(k,l)}
, consists in constructing the sequence
(c_{i})_{i\geq 1}
as follows:

(a)

for the unique
n\geq 1
such that
a^{(k,l)}_{n-1}\leq m<a^{(k,l)}_{n}
, set
c_{i}=0
for
i\geq n
,

(b)

if
c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n}
are set for any
1\leq i<n
, then set

c_{i}=\left\lfloor\frac{m-\sum_{j=i+1}^{n-1}c_{j}a^{(k,l)}_{j}}{a^{(k,l)}_{i}}\right\rfloor\,\cdot

(2)

When
(w_{i}^{(k,l)})_{i\geq 0}
is non-increasing, we have

(c_{i})\ll(d_{i})\Longrightarrow\Gamma_{(k,l)}((c_{i}))\leq\Gamma_{(k,l)}((d_{i}))\,,

(4.1.7)

and
\Gamma_{(k,l)}(_{n+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)})\subset(0;w_{n}^{(k,l)})
.

Another interpretation of the map
[\cdot]^{(k,l)}
is that the terms of the sequences are obtained by the greedy algorithm, as we
take
at each step
i
the maximal number of
a^{(k,l)}_{i}
from the remaining amount.

The infinite
(k,l)
-admissible words as defined before will find their use in the comprehension of
(k,l)
-Euler partitions. To deal with the
(k,l)
-lecture hall partitions, there is a need of defining analogous objects, viewed as the
finite

(k,l)
-admissible words.

Definition 4.8
.

For positive integer
n
, and all
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, define
\left[m\right]_{n}^{(k,l)}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
such that
c_{n}=\lfloor m/a^{(k,l)}_{n}\rfloor
, and

(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1},0,\ldots)=\left[m-c_{n}a^{(k,l)}_{n}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

(4.1.8)

Thus,
m=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}a^{(k,l)}_{i}
. Set
\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}=\left[\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right]^{(k,l)}_{n}
. One can check that
\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}
is in bijection with
{}^{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We then extend
\prec
to the set
\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}
with the following relation:

(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})\prec(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\text{ if and only if there exists }1\leq i\leq n\text{ such that }c_{i}<d_{i}\text{ and }c_{j}=d_{j}\text{ for }n\geq j>i\,\cdot

By writing

(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n})\equiv((c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1},0,\ldots),c_{n})\,,

we have

(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n})\prec(d_{1},\cdots,d_{n})
if and only
c_{n}<d_{n}
or
c_{n}=d_{n}
and
(c_{1},\ldots,d_{n-1},0,\ldots)\prec(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n-1},0,\ldots)
.
Hence, for
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the relation (
4.1.6
) then yields

r<s\Longleftrightarrow\left[r\right]^{(k,l)}_{n}\prec\left[s\right]^{(k,l)}_{n}\,\cdot

(4.1.9)

We also set by convention

\left[\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right]^{(k,l)}_{0}=\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{0}=\{\emptyset\}\cdot

For
1\leq i\leq n+1
, denote by
{}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}
the subset of
\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}
consisting of sequences with the
(i-1)
first terms equal to 0, and note that

\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}=\,_{1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}\supset\,_{2}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}\supset\cdots\supset\,_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}\supset\,_{n+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}=\{(0)_{j=1}^{n}\}\,\cdot

Remark that for
1\leq i\leq n
,
{}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}
is in bijection with
{}^{n}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
For
s\in\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}
, we also set the sequence
{}_{i}s
to be the sequence
s
where the
(i-1)
first terms are replaced by 0, and observe that, analogously to (
4.1.2
),

{}_{i}s=\max\{(c_{j})_{j=1}^{n}\in\,_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}:(c_{j})_{j=1}^{n}\preceq s\}\,\cdot

Example 4.9
.

For example, we enumerate in the following table the twenty first non-negative integers in terms of
(2,3)
and
(3,2)
-admissible words:

4.1.2.
The case
(k,1)

Recall (
2.1.2
). For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\begin{cases}a^{(1,k)}_{2n}=ka^{(k,1)}_{2n}=a_{n}^{(k-2)}\\
a^{(1,k)}_{2n-1}=a^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}=a_{n}^{(k-2)}+a_{n-1}^{(k-2)}\end{cases}\cdot

(4.1.10)

Our definition of
(k,1)
-admissible words is deeply linked to the
(k-2)
-admissible words.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
m
be a non negative integer. Set
\left[m\right]^{(k,1)}=\left[m\right]^{(k-2)}
. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, set
\left[m\right]^{(k,1)}_{2n}=\left[m\right]^{(k-2)}_{n}
, and set
\left[m\right]^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
such that
c_{n}=\lfloor m/a^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}\rfloor
, and

(1)

if
m-c_{n}a^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}<a_{n-1}^{(k-2)}
, then

(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1})=\left[m-c_{2n-1}a^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}\right]^{(k-2)}_{n-1}\,,

(2)

otherwise,
0\leq m-c_{n}a^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}-a_{n-1}^{(k-2)}<a_{n}^{(k-2)}
, and then

(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-2},c_{n-1}-1,0)=\left[m-c_{2n-1}a^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}-a_{n-1}^{(k-2)}\right]^{(k-2)}_{n}\,\cdot

In particular,
\left[m\right]^{(k,1)}_{1}=\left[m\right]^{(k,1)}_{2}=(m)
, and we set by convention
\left[m\right]^{(k,1)}_{0}=\emptyset
.
These definitions imply that, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

\left[m\right]^{(k,1)}_{n}=(c_{i})_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\Longrightarrow m=c_{\lceil n/2\rceil}a^{(k,1)}_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}c_{i}a_{i}^{(k-2)}\,\cdot

Denote by
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}
the set
\left[\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right]^{(k,1)}
and denote by
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}
the set
\left[\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right]^{(k,1)}_{n}
for all positive integer
n
. The sequences in these sets are called the
(k,1)
-admissible words. In particular,
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}=\mathcal{C}^{(k-2)}
and
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n}=\mathcal{C}^{(k-2)}_{n}
. Moreover, set
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}=\,^{1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}\sqcup\,^{0}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}
with

{}^{0}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}=\{(c_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}:c_{n-1}=0\}

and

{}^{1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}=\{(c_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}:c_{n-1}>0\}\,\cdot

Then,
{}^{0}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}
is in bijection with
{}^{n-1}\mathcal{C}^{(k-2)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with

(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-2},0,c_{n})\mapsto((c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-2},0,\ldots),c_{n})\,,

and

{}^{1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}
is in bijection with
{}^{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k-2)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with

(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-2},c_{n-1}+1,c_{n})\mapsto((c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1},0,\ldots),c_{n})\,\cdot

Note that
\prec
define a total strict order on
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}
and on
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}
, and by (
4.1.6
), we have the equivalence

r<s\Longleftrightarrow\left[r\right]^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}\prec\left[s\right]^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}\,\cdot

Finally, define
{}_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}=\,_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k-2)}
and for
1\leq i\leq\lceil n/2\rceil+1
,
{}_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}
the set of sequences of
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}
with the
(i-1)^{th}
first terms equal to 0.

Example 4.11
.

(6,1)
-admissible words:

\hline\cr 0&(0,0,\ldots)&(0,0,0)&(0,0,0)&10&(2,2,0,\ldots)&(2,2,0)&(2,2,0)\\

\hline\cr\end{array}

4.1.3.
The case
(1,k)

The definition of the
(1,k)
-admissible words dwells on the definition of
(k,1)
-admissible words and the properties of Lemma
2.5
, with
x=s^{(k,1)}_{0}
in
(1)
and
x=\frac{a^{(k,1)}_{n+1}}{a^{(1,k)}_{n}}
in
(2)
.

Definition 4.12
.

Set
\left[0\right]^{(1,k)}=(\left[0\right]^{(k,1)},\left[0\right]^{(k,1)})
, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}

\left[m\right]^{(1,k)}=\left(\left[\left\lfloor s^{(k,1)}_{0}\cdot m\right\rfloor+1\right]^{(k,1)},\left[\left\lceil s^{(k,1)}_{2}\cdot m\right\rceil-1\right]^{(k,1)}\right)\,,

and for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

\left[m\right]^{(1,k)}_{n}=\left(\left[\left\lceil\frac{a^{(k,1)}_{n+1}\cdot m}{a^{(1,k)}_{n}}\right\rceil\right]^{(k,1)}_{n+1},\left[\left\lfloor\frac{a^{(k,1)}_{n-1}\cdot m}{a^{(1,k)}_{n}}\right\rfloor\right]^{(k,1)}_{n-1}\right)\,\cdot

In particular,
\left[m\right]^{(1,k)}_{1}=((m),\emptyset)
.
Denote by
\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}
the set
\left[\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right]^{(1,k)}
and denote by
\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{n}
the set
\left[\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right]^{(1,k)}_{n}
for all positive integer
n
. The pair of sequences in these sets are called the
(1,k)
-admissible words.

.

4.2.
The transformation
0\cdot

In this part, we define a shifting transformation. This will allows us to investigate the ratio constraint in our partition in terms of shifting of the
(k,l)
-admissible words.

Definition 4.14
.

For
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
and all integer sequence
c=(c_{i})_{i=1}^{t}
,
0\cdot c
denotes the sequence
d=(d_{i})_{i=1}^{t+1}
satisfying
d_{1}=0
and
d_{i+1}=c_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq t
, and denote by
0\cdot
the function induced by the corresponding transformation.

Observe that for
c=\emptyset
,
0\cdot c=(0)
.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
k,l\geq 2
.
For positive integers
n
,
i
and
n+1\geq j\geq 1
,
0\cdot
induces a bijection from
{}_{i}^{n}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}
to
{}_{i+1}^{n+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
and a bijection from
{}_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}_{n}
to
{}_{j+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n+1}
which preserve the order
\prec
.

Let
k\geq 4
.
For positive integers
n,i
and
\lceil n/2\rceil+1\geq j\geq 1
, the transformation
0\cdot
induces a bijection from
{}_{i}^{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}
to
{}_{i+1}^{n+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}
and a bijection from
{}_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}
to
{}_{j+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n+2}
which preserve the order
\prec
.

Extend this transformation to the set of sequence pairs with
p_{i}(0\cdot s)=0\cdot p_{i}(s)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, set
{}_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}=\underbrace{0\cdots 0}_{d\text{ times}}\cdot\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}
, and
{}_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{n}=\underbrace{0\cdots 0}_{d\text{ times}}\cdot\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{n}
.
We conventionally extend
\preceq
on
{}_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}
such that for
u,v\in\,_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}
,

u\preceq v\Longleftrightarrow p_{i}(u)\preceq p_{i}(v)\text{ for }i\in\{1,2\}\,\cdot

(4.2.1)

In the same way, we extend
\preceq
on
\,{}_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{n}
. In particular, for
d=0
,
\preceq
defines an order on
\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}
and
\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{n}
.

4.3.
Followers and admissible words

This part is dedicated to the study of the relations between two
(k,l)
-admissible words consecutive in terms of
\preceq
.

4.3.1.
The case
k,l\geq 2

The next lemma relates consecutive
(k,l)
-admissible words for the infinite version.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and
(c_{i})_{i\geq 1}\in\,_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
. Let
(d_{i})=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)},(c_{i}))
. The following then occurs.

(1)

If there exists
j\geq n
such that
c_{i}=o^{(k,l)}_{i}+\delta_{i,j}
for
n\leq i\leq j
, then

0=d_{n}=\cdots=d_{j}\quad,\quad d_{j+1}=c_{j+1}+1\quad\text{and}\quad d_{i}=c_{i}\text{ for }i\geq j+2\,\cdot

(4.3.1)

(2)

Otherwise,

d_{n}=c_{n}+1\quad\text{and}\quad d_{i}=c_{i}\text{ for }i\geq n+1\,\cdot

(4.3.2)

We now state the analogous finite version of Lemma
4.16
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
n\geq j\geq 1
be positive integers and
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n}
. Let
(d_{i})=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{n},(c_{i}))
. The following then occurs.

(1)

If there exists
j\leq h\leq n-1
such that
c_{i}=o^{(k,l)}_{i}+\delta_{i,h}
for
j\leq i\leq h
, then

0=d_{j}=\cdots=d_{h}\quad,\quad d_{h+1}=c_{h+1}+1\quad\text{and}\quad d_{i}=c_{i}\text{ for }h+2\leq i\leq n\,\cdot

(4.3.3)

(2)

Otherwise,

d_{j}=c_{j}+1\quad\text{and}\quad d_{i}=c_{i}\text{ for }j+1\leq i\leq n\,\cdot

(4.3.4)

Examples 4.18
.

We have the following.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{2}\mathcal{C}^{(2,3)},(0,0,1,1,0,\ldots))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{3}\mathcal{C}^{(2,3)},(0,0,1,1,0,\ldots))=(0,0,0,0,1,0,\ldots)\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{2}\mathcal{C}^{(2,3)}_{4},(0,0,2,1))

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,2)\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{1}\mathcal{C}^{(2,3)}_{4},(0,0,2,1))

\displaystyle=(1,0,2,1)\,\cdot

These lemmas allows us to relate the integers corresponding to consecutive
(k,l)
-admissible words and to present the connection to the ratio constraint.

Proposition 4.19
.

For positive integer
n
, we have the following.

(1)

For
n\geq 1
, if
(d_{i})_{i\geq 1}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)},(c_{i})_{i\geq 1})
,
one of following then holds:

\displaystyle(d_{i})_{i\geq n}\in\,_{n}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}\setminus\,_{n+1}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\sum_{i=n}^{\infty}(d_{i}-c_{i})a^{(k,l)}_{i+1}=a^{(k,l)}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow 0\leq\sum_{i\geq n}c_{i}a^{(k,l)}_{i+1}-s_{0}^{(k,l)}\sum_{i\geq n}c_{i}a^{(l,k)}_{i}\leq s_{n}^{(k,l)}-s_{n+1}^{(k,l)}\,,

(4.3.5)

\displaystyle(d_{i})_{i\geq n}\in\,_{n+1}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\sum_{i=n}^{\infty}(d_{i}-c_{i})a^{(k,l)}_{i+1}=a^{(k,l)}_{n+1}-a^{(l,k)}_{n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow s_{n}^{(k,l)}-s_{n+1}^{(k,l)}<\sum_{i\geq n}c_{i}a^{(k,l)}_{i+1}-s_{0}^{(k,l)}\sum_{i\geq n}c_{i}a^{(l,k)}_{i}\leq s_{n}^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

(4.3.6)

(2)

For
2n-1\geq j\geq 1
, if
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{2n-1}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}_{2n-1},(c_{i})_{i\geq 1})
,
one of following holds:

\displaystyle(d_{i})_{i=1}^{2n-1}\in\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}\setminus\,_{j+1}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\sum_{i=j}^{2n-1}(d_{i}-c_{i})a^{(k,l)}_{i+1}=a^{(k,l)}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow 0\leq a^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}\sum_{i=j}^{2n-1}c_{i}a^{(k,l)}_{i+1}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n}\sum_{i=j}^{2n-1}c_{i}a^{(l,k)}_{i}<a^{(k,l)}_{2n-j}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n-1-j}\,,

(4.3.7)

\displaystyle(d_{i})_{i=1}^{2n-1}\in\,_{j+1}\mathcal{C}^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\sum_{i=j}^{2n-1}(d_{i}-c_{i})a^{(k,l)}_{i+1}=a^{(k,l)}_{j+1}-a^{(k,l)}_{j}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow a^{(k,l)}_{2n-j}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n-1-j}\leq a^{(l,k)}_{2n-1}\sum_{i=j}^{2n-1}c_{i}a^{(k,l)}_{i+1}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n}\sum_{i=j}^{2n-1}c_{i}a^{(l,k)}_{i}<a^{(k,l)}_{2n-j}\,\cdot

(4.3.8)

(3)

For
2n\geq j\geq 1
, if
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{2n}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{2n},(c_{i})_{i=1}^{2n})
,
one of following holds:

\displaystyle(d_{i})_{i}^{2n}\in\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{2n}\setminus\,_{j+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{2n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\sum_{i=j}^{2n}(d_{i}-c_{i})a^{(l,k)}_{i+1}=a^{(l,k)}_{j+1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow 0\leq a^{(k,l)}_{2n}\sum_{i=j}^{2n}c_{i}a^{(l,k)}_{i+1}-a^{(l,k)}_{2n+1}\sum_{i=j}^{2n}c_{i}a^{(k,l)}_{i}<a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1-j}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n-j}\,,

(4.3.9)

\displaystyle(d_{i})_{i}^{2n}\in\,_{j+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{2n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\sum_{i=j}^{2n}(d_{i}-c_{i})a^{(l,k)}_{i+1}=a^{(l,k)}_{j+1}-a^{(l,k)}_{j}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1-j}-a^{(k,l)}_{2n-j}\leq a^{(k,l)}_{2n}\sum_{i=j}^{2n}c_{i}a^{(l,k)}_{i+1}-a^{(l,k)}_{2n+1}\sum_{i=j}^{2n}c_{i}a^{(k,l)}_{i}<a^{(k,l)}_{2n+1-j}\,\cdot

(4.3.10)

4.3.2.
The cases
(k,1)

We proceed similarly to the case
(k,l)
with
k,l\geq 2
. As
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}=\mathcal{C}^{(k-2)}
and
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n}=\mathcal{C}^{(k-2)}_{n}
, we only discuss in the next lemma the notion on succession in
\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
n\geq j\geq 1
be positive integers and
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}
. Let
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{2n-1}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{2n-1},(c_{i})_{i=1}^{2n-1})
. The following then occurs.

(1)

If there exists
j\leq h\leq n-1
such that
c_{i}=o^{(k-2)}_{i}+\delta_{i,h}+\delta_{i,n-1}
for
j\leq i\leq h
, then

0=d_{j}=\cdots=d_{h}\quad,\quad d_{h+1}=c_{h+1}+1\quad\text{and}\quad d_{i}=c_{i}\text{ for }h+2\leq i\leq n\,\cdot

(4.3.11)

(2)

Otherwise,

d_{j}=c_{j}+1\quad\text{and}\quad d_{i}=c_{i}\text{ for }j+1\leq i\leq n\,\cdot

(4.3.12)

The proposition analogous to Proposition
4.19
is the following.

Proposition 4.21
.

For positive integer
n
, and
for
\lfloor n/2\rfloor\geq j\geq 1
,
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil},(d_{i})_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\in\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}

such that
(d_{i})_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n},(c_{i})_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil})
,
one of following holds:

\displaystyle(d_{i})_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\in\,_{j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}\setminus\,_{j+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow 0\leq a^{(k,1)}_{n+2}\sum_{i=j}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}c_{i}a^{(k,1)}_{2i+2}-a^{(k,1)}_{n}\sum_{i=j}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}c_{i}a^{(k,1)}_{2i}<a^{(k,1)}_{n+2-2j}-a^{(k,1)}_{n-2j}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow(d_{\lceil n/2\rceil}-c_{\lceil n/2\rceil})a^{(k,1)}_{n+2}+\sum_{i=j}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}(d_{i}-c_{i})a^{(k,1)}_{2i+2}=a^{(k,1)}_{2j+2}\,,

(4.3.13)

\displaystyle(d_{i})_{i=1}^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\in\,_{j+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow a^{(k,1)}_{n+2-2j}-a^{(k,1)}_{n-2j}\leq a^{(k,1)}_{n+2}\sum_{i=j}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}c_{i}a^{(k,1)}_{2i+2}-a^{(k,1)}_{n}\sum_{i=j}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}c_{i}a^{(k,1)}_{2i}<a^{(k,1)}_{n+2-2j}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow(d_{\lceil n/2\rceil}-c_{\lceil n/2\rceil})a^{(k,1)}_{n+2}+\sum_{i=j}^{\lceil n/2\rceil-1}(d_{i}-c_{i})a^{(k,1)}_{2i+2}=a^{(k,1)}_{2j+2}-a^{(k,1)}_{2j}\,\cdot

(4.3.14)

4.4.
Relations between admissible words, lecture-hall partitions and Euler partitions

4.4.1.
The case
k,l\geq 2

We now state the proposition that relates the
(k,l)
-admissible words and the shifting operation to the
(k,l)
-Euler partitions and
(k,l)
-lecture hall partitions.

Proposition 4.22
.

(1)

For
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

r>s_{0}^{(k,l)}s\Longleftrightarrow\,_{2}\left[r\right]^{(k,l)}=0\cdot\left[\lceil s_{2}^{(l,k)}r\rceil-1\right]^{(l,k)}\succeq 0\cdot\left[s\right]^{(l,k)}=\left[1+\lfloor s_{0}^{(k,l)}s\rfloor\right]^{(k,l)}\cdot

(4.4.1)

Hence, for a sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2t})
such that
t\geq 1
,
0=\lambda_{2t}\leq\lambda_{2t-1}
and
\lambda_{i}>0
for
1\leq i\leq 2t-2
,

\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(k,l)}\succeq 0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(l,k)}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq t-1\,,\\
\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(l,k)}\succeq 0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2i+1}\right]^{(k,l)}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq t-1\,\cdot\end{cases}

(4.4.2)

(2)

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. For
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

r\geq\frac{a^{(k,l)}_{n+1}}{a^{(l,k)}_{n}}s\Longleftrightarrow\,_{2}\left[r\right]^{(k,l)}_{n+1}=0\cdot\left[\left\lfloor\frac{a^{(l,k)}_{n}}{a^{(k,l)}_{n+1}}r\right\rfloor\right]^{(l,k)}_{n}\succeq 0\cdot\left[s\right]^{(l,k)}_{n}=\left[\left\lceil\frac{a^{(k,l)}_{n+1}}{a^{(l,k)}_{n}}s\right\rceil\right]^{(k,l)}_{n+1}\,\cdot

(4.4.3)

Hence, for a sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2n-1})
of non-negative integers,

\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{2n-1}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(k,l)}_{2i}\succeq 0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(l,k)}_{2i-1}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq n-1\,\,\\
\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(l,k)}_{2i-1}\succeq 0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2i-2}\right]^{(k,l)}_{2i-2}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq n-1\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.4)

and for a sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2n})
of non-negative integers,

\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}_{2n}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(k,l)}_{2i}\succeq 0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(l,k)}_{2i-1}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq n\,\,\\
\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(l,k)}_{2i-1}\succeq 0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2i-2}\right]^{(k,l)}_{2i-2}\quad\text{for}\quad 1\leq i\leq n-1\,\cdot\end{cases}

(4.4.5)

4.4.2.
The cases
(k,1)
and
(1,k)

We first present the relation between the shift operation and the ratio constraint for
(k,1)
-admissible words.

Proposition 4.23
.

For
k\geq 4
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

r\geq\frac{a^{(k,1)}_{n+2}}{a^{(k,1)}_{n}}s\Longleftrightarrow\,_{2}\left[r\right]^{(k,1)}_{n+2}=0\cdot\left[\left\lfloor\frac{a^{(k,1)}_{n}}{a^{(k,1)}_{n+2}}r\right\rfloor\right]^{(k,1)}_{n}\succeq 0\cdot\left[s\right]^{(k,1)}_{n}=\left[\left\lceil\frac{a^{(k,1)}_{n+2}}{a^{(k,1)}_{n}}s\right\rceil\right]^{(k,1)}_{n+2}\,\cdot

(4.4.6)

In the following lemma, we rewrite Lemma
2.5
in terms of
(1,k)
-admissible words, and present the corresponding properties related to the succession.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
d,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.

(1)

We have

_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}=\left\{(s_{1},s_{2})\in(\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)})^{2}:0\cdot s_{2}\preceq s_{1}\preceq 0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)},s_{2})\right\}\,\cdot

(4.4.7)

Therefore, the sequence
(t_{1},t_{2})=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)},(s_{1},s_{2}))
satisfies the following:

(a)

if
s_{1}\neq 0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)},s_{2})
, then
t_{2}=s_{2}
and
t_{1}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)},s_{1})
,

(b)

if
s_{1}=0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)},s_{2})
, then
t_{2}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)},s_{2})
and
t_{1}=s_{1}
.

Furthermore, if
(t_{1},t_{2})=\mathcal{F}(m,\,_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)},(s_{1},s_{2}))
, then

m=m_{1}+2m_{2}-\chi(t_{1}=0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)},t_{2}))+\chi(s_{1}=0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)},s_{2}))\,,

(4.4.8)

where

\displaystyle m_{1}

\displaystyle=\sharp\left\{r\in\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}\setminus\,_{d+2}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}:s_{1}\preceq r\prec t_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle m_{2}

\displaystyle=\sharp\left\{r\in\,_{d+2}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}:s_{1}\preceq r\prec t_{1}\right\}\,\cdot

(2)

When
(d,n)\neq(0,1)
, we have

_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{n}=\left\{(s_{1},s_{2})\in\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n+1+2d}\times\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n-1+2d}:0\cdot s_{2}\preceq s_{1}\preceq 0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n-1+2d},s_{2})\right\}\,\cdot

(4.4.9)

Therefore, the sequence
(t_{1},t_{2})=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{n},(s_{1},s_{2}))
satisfies the following:

(a)

if
s_{1}\neq 0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n-1+2d},s_{2})
, then
t_{2}=s_{2}
and
t_{1}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n+1+2d},s_{1})
,

(b)

if
s_{1}=0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n-1+2d},s_{2})
, then
t_{2}=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n-1+2d},s_{2})
and
t_{1}=s_{1}
.

Furthermore, if
(t_{1},t_{2})=\mathcal{F}(m,\,_{+d}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{n},(s_{1},s_{2}))
, then

m=m_{1}+2m_{2}-\chi(t_{1}=0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n-1+2d},t_{2}))+\chi(s_{1}=0\cdot\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n-1+2d},s_{2}))\,,

(4.4.10)

where

\displaystyle m_{1}

\displaystyle=\sharp\left\{r\in\,_{d+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n+1+2d}\setminus\,_{d+2}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n+1+2d}:s_{1}\prec r\preceq t_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle m_{2}

\displaystyle=\sharp\left\{r\in\,_{d+2}\mathcal{C}^{(k,1)}_{n+1+2d}:s_{1}\prec r\preceq t_{1}\right\}\,\cdot

(3)

When
(d,n)=(0,1)
,

{}_{+0}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{1}=\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{1}=\{(s_{1},s_{2}):s_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},s_{2}=\emptyset\}\,\cdot

(4.4.11)

Therefore, the sequence
(t_{1},t_{2})=\mathcal{F}(1,\,_{+0}\mathcal{C}^{(1,k)}_{1},(s_{1},s_{2}))
is such that
t_{1}=s_{1}+1
and
t_{2}=s_{2}=\emptyset
.

The characterization of
(k,1)
and
(1,k)
-Euler and lecture hall partitions in terms of
(k,1)
and
(1,k)
-admissible words is the following.

Proposition 4.25
.

For
k\geq 4
, we have the following.

(1)

For
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle r>s^{(k,1)}_{0}\cdot s

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\,\left[r\right]^{(k,1)}\succeq p_{1}(\left[s\right]^{(1,k)})\,,

(4.4.12)

\displaystyle r>s^{(1,k)}_{0}\cdot s

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\,p_{2}(\left[r\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq\left[s\right]^{(k,1)}\,\cdot

(4.4.13)

Hence, for a sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2t})
such that
t\geq 1
,
0=\lambda_{2t}\leq\lambda_{2t-1}
and
\lambda_{i}>0
for
1\leq i\leq 2t-2
,

\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,1)}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(k,1)}\succeq p_{1}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq t\,,\\
p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)\succeq\left[\lambda_{2i+1}\right]^{(k,1)}\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq t-1\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.14)

\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(1,k)}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(k,1)}\succeq p_{1}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i+1}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq t-1\,,\\
p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)\succeq\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(k,1)}\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq t\,\cdot\end{cases}

(4.4.15)

(2)

For
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle r\geq\frac{a^{(k,1)}_{n+1}}{a^{(1,k)}_{n}}s

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\,\left[r\right]^{(k,1)}_{n+1}\succeq p_{1}(\left[s\right]^{(1,k)}_{n})\,,

(4.4.16)

\displaystyle r\geq\frac{a^{(1,k)}_{n+1}}{a^{(k,1)}_{n}}s

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\,p_{2}(\left[r\right]^{(1,k)}_{n+1})\succeq\left[s\right]^{(k,1)}_{n}\,,

(4.4.17)

Hence, for a sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2n})
of non-negative integers,

\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,1)}_{2n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(k,1)}_{2i}\succeq p_{1}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}\right)\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq n\,,\\
p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i+1}\right]^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}\right)\succeq\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(k,1)}_{2i}\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq n-1\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.18)

\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(1,k)}_{2n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i+1}\right]^{(k,1)}_{2i+1}\succeq p_{1}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(1,k)}_{2i}\right)\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq n-1\,,\\
p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(1,k)}_{2i}\right)\succeq\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq n\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.19)

and for a sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{2n-1})
of non-negative integers,

\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,1)}_{2n-1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(k,1)}_{2i}\succeq p_{1}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(1,k)}_{2i-1}\right)\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq n-1\,,\\
p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i+1}\right]^{(1,k)}_{2i+1}\right)\succeq\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(k,1)}_{2i}\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq n-1\,,\end{cases}

(4.4.20)

\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(1,k)}_{2n-1}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}\left[\lambda_{2i+1}\right]^{(k,1)}_{2i+1}\succeq p_{1}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(1,k)}_{2i}\right)\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq n-1\,,\\
p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2i}\right]^{(1,k)}_{2i}\right)\succeq\left[\lambda_{2i-1}\right]^{(k,1)}_{2i-1}\ \ \text{for}\ \ 1\leq i\leq n-1\,\cdot\end{cases}

(4.4.21)

5.
Well-definedness of
\Phi^{(k,l)}

We first give an interpretation of the insertion of the parts
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
into the pairs
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
in terms of the admissible words. Denote by
(\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)},\lambda_{2j}^{(i)})
the pairs
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
after the insertion of all the parts
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
, and let
m_{i}^{(j)}
be the number of parts
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
inserted into the pair
(\lambda_{2j-1},\lambda_{2j})
. Note that
m_{i}^{(1)}
equals the number of occurrences of
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
in
\nu
, and the image by
\Phi^{(k,l)}
consists of
(\lambda_{j}^{(1)})_{j=1}^{2t}
, where
t
is the smallest
j
such that
\lambda_{2j}^{(1)}=0
.

5.1.
The case
k,l\geq 2

We now state the key proposition that induces the well-definedness of
\Phi^{(k,l)}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

For
t\geq j\geq 1
,

(1)

for
i\geq 2
, we have

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}=0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(i)}\right]^{(l,k)}\in\,_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

(5.1.1)

(a)

Furthermore,

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}=\mathcal{F}\left(m_{i}^{(j)},\,_{i}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)},\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\right)\,\cdot

(b)

Reciprocally,

{}_{i+1}\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}=\mathcal{F}\left(m_{i-1}^{(j+1)},\,_{i+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)},\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\right)\,\cdot

(2)

Finally,
\lambda_{2j}^{(1)}=\lambda_{2j}^{(2)}
,

(a)

and
\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(1)}\right]^{(k,l)}=\mathcal{F}\left(m_{1}^{(j)},\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)},\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(2)}\right]^{(k,l)}\right)\,\cdot

(b)

Reciprocally,

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(1)}\right]^{(k,l)}=\mathcal{F}\left(m_{1}^{(j)},\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)},0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(1)}\right]^{(l,k)}\right)\,\cdot

Examples 5.2
.

We illustrate the previous proposition with Examples
3.2
.

with

Note that for
i\geq 1
,
m_{i}^{(1)}
recursively derives the numbers
m_{i+1-j}^{(j)}
and the pairs
(\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1-j)},\lambda_{2j}^{(i+1-j)})
for
1\leq j\leq i
.
The next figure gives an interpretation of the relation between the parts at intermediate phases.

{}_{i+1}\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}
\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1)}\right]^{(k,l)}
\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}
\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}

By Lemmas
2.7
and
4.15
, in
{}_{i+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
,

00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}\preceq\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\Longleftrightarrow 00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i-1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\preceq\,_{i+1}\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

By (
4.1.2
), this yields

00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}\preceq\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\Longleftrightarrow 00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i-1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\preceq\,\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

.

5.1.1.
Proof that
\Phi^{(k,l)}(\mathcal{B}^{(k,l)})\subset\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}

By (
5.1.1
) and fact
(2)
, for
j\geq 1
,

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\succeq\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(2)}\right]^{(k,l)}=0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2)}\right]^{(l,k)}=0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(1)}\right]^{(l,k)}\,\cdot

By (
4.4.2
), to prove that
\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}
, it suffices to show that to show that

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(2)}\right]^{(k,l)}=0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2)}\right]^{(l,k)}=0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(1)}\right]^{(l,k)}\succeq 00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

For
n
the greatest index
i
such that
b^{(k,l)}_{i}
occurs in
\nu
, we have for
1\leq j\leq n+1
that

\lambda_{2j-1}^{(n+2-j)}=\lambda_{2j}^{(n+2-j)}=0
, and

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(n+2-j)}\right]^{(k,l)}=00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(n+1-j)}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

Thus, by (
5.1.2
), by induction on
i
for
n\geq i>j\geq 1
,

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1-j)}\right]^{(k,l)}\succeq 00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i-j)}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

In particular, for
i=j+1
, we obtain the result. Note that
\lambda_{2n}^{(1)}=0
, and by the choice of
t
, we have
t
is at most equal to
n
.

5.1.2.
Existence and the uniqueness of the reverse image

Let
\lambda
be a sequence in
\mathcal{L}^{(k,l)}
with
2t
parts, and suppose that the first part
\lambda_{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}_{2n+1}
. Since by (
4.4.2
),

(0)_{i\geq 1}=\underbrace{0\cdots 0}_{2t-1}\left[\lambda_{2t}\right]^{(l,k)}\preceq\underbrace{0\cdots 0}_{2t-2}\left[\lambda_{2t-1}\right]^{(k,l)}\preceq\cdots\preceq 0\cdot\left[\lambda_{2}\right]^{(l,k)}\preceq\left[\lambda_{1}\right]^{(k,l)}\prec(\delta_{i,2n+1})_{i\geq 1}\,

we necessarily have by Lemma
4.15
that
\left[\lambda_{2j-1}\right]^{(k,l)}\prec(\delta_{i,2n+3-2j})_{i\geq 1}
.
Let us set
\lambda_{j}^{(1)}=\lambda_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq 2t
.
By considering the pair
(\lambda_{2t-1}^{(1)},\lambda_{2t}^{(1)})
, we have
m_{1}^{(t)}=\lambda_{2t-1}^{(1)}
and
m_{i}^{(t)}=0
for
1<i
. Suppose now that, for some
1\leq j\leq t-1
, we retrieve
\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i)}
for
i\geq 1
(and then
m_{i}^{(j+1)}
by fact
(1.a)
and
(2)
).

(1)

We retrieve
\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(2)}\right]^{(k,l)}
and
m_{1}^{(j)}
from fact
(2)
, and we have by (
5.1.1
) that

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(2)}\right]^{(k,l)}=0\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2)}\right]^{(l,k)}=0\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(1)}\right]^{(l,k)}\succeq 00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(2)}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

(2)

Suppose that we retrieve for
\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}
for
i\geq 2
, and that in
{}_{i+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
,

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}\succeq 00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i-1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

Since
00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i-1)}\right]^{(k,l)}
admits a
(m_{i-1}^{(j+1)})^{th}
predecessor
00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}
in
{}_{i+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
, by Lemma
2.7
,
\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}
also admits a
(m_{i-1}^{(j+1)})^{th}
predecessor in
{}_{i+1}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
, namely
\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1)}\right]^{(k,l)}
(by fact
(1.b)
), which satisfies

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i+1)}\right]^{(k,l)}\succeq 00\cdot\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)}\,\cdot

(3)

Finally, by (
5.1.1
),
\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(2n+3-2j)}\right]^{(k,l)}\in\,_{2n+3-2j}\mathcal{C}^{(k,l)}
, and since
(\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}\right]^{(k,l)})_{i\geq 1}
is non-increasing in terms of
\preceq
, we necessarily have that

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(2n+3-2j)}\right]^{(k,l)}\prec(\delta_{i,2n+3-2j})_{i\geq 1}
, so that
\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)}=0
for
i\geq 2n+3-2j
.

Recursively on
i
, we obtain the datum
(\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)})_{i\geq 1}
. Remark that, by (
5.1.1
) and fact
(2)
, the datum
(\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)})_{i\geq 2}
allows us to retrieve
(\lambda_{2j}^{(i)})_{i\geq 2}
, and fact
(1)
,
(\lambda_{2j-1}^{(i)})_{i\geq 1}
allows us to recover
(m_{i}^{(j)})_{i\geq 1}
.
Finally, we recover the datum
(\lambda_{1}^{(i)})_{i\geq 1}
and then
(m_{i}^{(1)})_{i\geq 1}
and we conclude.

\displaystyle\left[\mu_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)}\succeq 0\cdot\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow p_{u}(\left[\mu_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq 0\cdot p_{u}(\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)})\quad\text{for}\quad u\in\{1,2\}\,,

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}p_{1}(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq 0\cdot p_{1}(\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)})\\
{}_{i}p_{2}(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq 0\cdot p_{2}(\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)})\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}0\cdot p_{2}(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq 0\cdot p_{1}(\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)})\\
p_{2}(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq 0\cdot p_{2}(\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)})\end{cases}

As

p_{2}(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq p_{1}(\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)})\Longrightarrow p_{2}(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq 0\cdot p_{2}(\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)})\,,

we then have

\left[\mu_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)}\succeq 0\cdot\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)}\Longleftrightarrow p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)\succeq p_{1}\left(\left[\mu_{2j+2}^{(2i-2)}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)\,\cdot

p_{2}(\left[\mu_{2j}^{(2i)}\right]^{(1,k)})\succeq\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(2i-1)}\right]^{(k,1)}\end{cases}

we then have

5.2.1.
Proof that
\Phi^{(k,1)}(\mathcal{B}^{(k,1)})\subset\mathcal{L}^{(k,1)}

By facts
(2)
and
(3)
, for

,

\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(1)}\right]^{(k,1)}\succeq\left[\lambda_{2j-1}^{(2)}\right]^{(k,1)}=p_{1}\left(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2)}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)=p_{1}\left(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2)}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)\,\cdot

By (
4.4.14
), to prove that
\lambda\in\mathcal{L}^{(k,1)}
, it suffices to show that to show that

p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(1)}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)=p_{2}\left(\left[\lambda_{2j}^{(2)}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)=p_{2}\left(\left[\mu_{2j}^{(2)}\right]^{(1,k)}\right)\succeq\left[\lambda_{2j+1}^{(1)}\right]^{(k,1)}\,\cdot

For
n\geq 1
such that
